The next four are copy-number calls on non-coding or poorly characterized genes, so their ranking is less reliable.

**Top 5 by altered samples (mutations + amplifications/deep deletions; no structural variants are recorded for this study)**

| Rank | Gene | Altered samples | Breakdown | Share of profiled samples* |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TP53 | 38 | 32 mutated, 6 CNA (1 amp, 5 deep del) | 38/143 = 26.6% (mutation-profiled) |

| 5 | RN7SL442P | 31 | 31 amplified | 31/81 = 38.3% (CNA-profiled) |

Note: TRIM16 is tied at 31 with the rank 5 gene, and about 15 other genes sit at 30 samples.

**Caveats**
- **Mixed denominators.** Only 143 samples have mutation data and only 81 have copy-number data. 66 have both. The percentages above use different denominators, so they are not directly comparable.
- **Same-denominator check.** When I restricted all calls to the 66 samples profiled for both, the top genes were LINC00901 (26/66), RNU5E-8P (25), TRPS1 (25), and TRPS1-AS1 (25). TP53 did not appear in the top 8.
- **Likely CNA artifact.** Dozens of unrelated genes, mostly non-coding, share nearly identical counts of 30–32 altered samples. This pattern suggests segment-level copy-number calls spanning neighboring regions rather than independent gene-specific events. This is my reading of the data, not something the database confirms, so treat the copy-number ranks with caution.
- **Counting unit.** These are sample-level counts.

**Reliable answer:** TP53 is the most frequently altered gene (38 samples). Mutations account for 32 of those, and each mutation is a single-sample event.

#23 AnalysisCohort & clinical counts · msk_chord_2024What is the correlation between tumor mutational burden and microsatellite instability status in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD Study? ✗27s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Tumor mutational burden (TMB) demonstrates a significant positive association with microsatellite instability (MSI) in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD cohort. Using continuous MSI scores, TMB correlates moderately with MSI, with a Spearman coefficient of 0.37 (p = 1.18 × 10⁻¹⁷⁵) and a Pearson coefficient of 0.58 (p = 0.00), indicating that higher MSI scores correspond to higher mutational burden. Consistent with these quantitative correlations, categorical MSI typing shows a clear separation wherein MSI-instable tumors exhibit high TMB, MSI-stable tumors show uniformly low TMB, and indeterminate cases fall between these groups.

I'll check the relevant guides and the MSK-CHORD study details first.## TMB by MSI status in colorectal cancer (MSK-CHORD)

MSI-Instable colorectal tumors have a much higher tumor mutational burden (TMB) than MSI-Stable tumors. The two groups barely overlap in the descriptive summary. I could not compute a correlation coefficient or p-value here, so the formal test is left for you to run (see below).

Counts are samples, using the `MSI_TYPE` and `TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS` (mutations/Mb) attributes, for patients whose `CANCER_TYPE` is Colorectal Cancer (5,584 samples with MSI data).

| MSI_TYPE | Samples | % of CRC samples | Mean TMB | Median TMB | Range |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stable | 4,712 | 84.4% | 7.58 | 5.87 | 0.00 – 570.96 |
| Instable | 588 | 10.5% | 64.62 | 56.68 | 0.00 – 490.57 |
| Indeterminate | 190 | 3.4% | 18.40 | 6.56 | 1.64 – 343.53 |
| Do not report | 54 | 1.0% | 6.96 | 3.39 | 0.00 – 74.31 |
| Missing MSI status | 40 | 0.7% | 8.02 | 3.69 | 0.00 – 95.12 |

**Observations**

- The median TMB of MSI-Instable tumors (56.7) is about 10-fold that of MSI-Stable tumors (5.9).
- MSI-Stable tumors have a few very high TMB values (up to 571), so the mean is pulled upward. Medians are the more reliable summary for this skewed data.
- MSI-Indeterminate tumors have intermediate TMB (median 6.6), with a wide range.

**Testing the association**

Because TMB is skewed, a rank-based test is the appropriate choice. The portal's Group Comparison tab has the same defaults, so you can run the comparison there:

- [Compare MSI-Instable vs MSI-Stable CRC samples (clinical tab)](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/clinical?comparisonId=6b00417f0e590df336307309)

The page reports 586 Instable and 4,682 Stable patients, which differ slightly from the sample counts above because the portal counts patients.

For a formal test, use a Wilcoxon rank-sum test on TMB between the two MSI groups. For a correlation coefficient, use Spearman's rho, treating MSI status as an ordinal or binary variable. In R, `wilcox.test(tmb ~ msi)` or `cor.test(tmb, msi_numeric, method = "spearman")`. In Python, `scipy.stats.mannwhitneyu` or `scipy.stats.spearmanr`. Exclude the "Do not report" and missing groups from the test.

“Across patients from the Lung Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas) study, EGFR mutations were not associated with a statistically significant difference in overall survival compared with EGFR–wild-type tumors (log-rank p = 0.125). Median overall survival was 42.5 months for EGFR-mutant patients versus 53.3 months for wild-type patients, with an estimated hazard ratio (HR) of 1.38 (95% CI: 0.87–2.18), indicating no clear survival disadvantage. **Per-mutation VAF** (tumor alt / (alt + ref) read counts)

| Sample | Gene | Protein change | Alt / Ref reads | VAF |
|---|---|---|---|---|

**What the distribution suggests**
- **Within-sample comparisons are the most informative.** In TCGA-AX-A0J0-01, the NRAS E162* VAF (0.10) is far below the KRAS K176Q VAF (0.42), which fits the NRAS event being subclonal relative to KRAS. In TCGA-A5-A0G2-01, the two NRAS calls (0.29 and 0.21) sit below the KRAS Q61H call (0.52), which points the same way.
- **In the other two samples the VAFs are similar** (TCGA-B5-A0JV-01: 0.20 vs 0.19; TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01: 0.22 vs 0.25), so there is no basis in these data to rank the events.
- **Only one call is near 0.5** (KRAS Q61H in TCGA-A5-A0G2-01). A VAF that high is compatible with a clonal, heterozygous event at high tumor purity, but it could also reflect copy-number changes, so it is not proof of clonality.

**Caveats**
- Raw VAFs are not adjusted for tumor purity or local copy number. Adjusted cancer cell fractions would be needed for a proper clonal call, and this database does not store them for these mutations.
- Four samples is too few to describe a distribution. These numbers are descriptive only.

**Method:** Somatic mutations only, across the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies (`pan_cancer_tcga`). Frequency = altered patients/samples ÷ samples profiled for that gene. Cancer types with fewer than 50 profiled samples are excluded. Copy-number and structural variants are not included, so these numbers will be lower than the portal's default view, which includes them.

*Non-small cell lung combines LUAD and LUSC.

**Observations**
- **Endometrial cancer** has the highest frequency for all four genes. Melanoma and bladder are also consistently high.
- **Colorectal cancer** stands out for ATM (13.1%), more than for BRCA1 or BRCA2.
- **Breast cancer**, which is often discussed in the context of BRCA1/2, is low here (2–3%) for BRCA1, BRCA2 and ATM, and under 1% for CHEK2.
- **Caveat:** Large genes such as ATM and BRCA2 are frequently mutated in hypermutated tumors (MSI and POLE-mutant endometrial, colorectal, melanoma, bladder). Their high frequency in those types may partly reflect a high overall mutation burden rather than selection for these genes. Testing this would require a comparison that accounts for mutation burden.

Older patients (>4) had significantly worse overall survival compared to younger patients (≤4) with a higher hazard of death (HR = 1.47; 95% CI 1.17–1.86; log-rank p = 3.37×10⁻⁴). Their median overall survival was 79.0 months (95% CI 53–122) whereas median survival was not reached in the ≤4 group.

#71 Out of scopeOut of scope · All StudiesWhat is the current and future support for storing and analyzing germline variants in cBioPortal, compared to other alternatives? ✗16s
Notes
A correct answer must: explain that cBioPortal supports germline variants (e.g. mutation_status GERMLINE, germline-only studies) and that most features (clinical correlation, study view, plots, group comparison) work the same as for somatic data; point to docs on building a germline study. Must not: describe cBioPortal as somatic-only.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/20

Unified agent · ✗ Fail 16s · 247,431 tok · $0.005 · 4 LLM / 4 tool calls · trace

**Short answer:** cBioPortal currently stores and visualizes germline variants, but only a small minority of its studies include them, and its analysis tools are built mainly for somatic data. I can't see a roadmap from the database, so I can't say what future support will look like.

**Current support (from the database and the germline guide)**

- **Limited coverage:** 16 of the 553 studies with mutation data have germline mutation calls. The largest are `lung_nci_2022` (72 samples), `pog570_bcgsc_2020` (68), `hnsc_a5consortium_2025` (66), `coadread_tcga_pub` (59), and `brca_tcga_pub` (58). Most others have fewer than 40 samples.
- **Storage:** Germline calls are stored alongside somatic ones, labeled in `mutation_status`. Labels vary by study (`Germline`, `GERMLINE`, `germline`), so comparisons must be case-insensitive. Many studies leave somatic calls unannotated (`NA`, `UNKNOWN`), which means unknown, not somatic.
- **Works for both variant types:** mutation frequency, protein domain views, clinical correlation, gene panel coverage, OncoPrint, and the mutation diagrams.
- **Designed mainly for somatic data:** driver annotations, OncoKB, mutual exclusivity analysis, and TMB. Germline calls are usually not annotated with driver status, and cBioPortal does not store ACMG pathogenicity classes in standard fields. Pathogenic, likely pathogenic, and VUS calls therefore have to be interpreted outside the portal.
- **Access varies:** Some public instances may exclude germline variants for privacy reasons, so germline data availability depends on the instance.

**Comparison with alternatives (general knowledge, not from cBioPortal data)**

- **ClinVar, gnomAD, and the ACMG/AMP framework** are better suited for variant classification and population frequencies. cBioPortal does not replace them for that purpose.
- **Dedicated germline or hereditary cancer platforms** (for example, institutional clinical-genetics databases) handle pathogenicity, family history, and reporting workflows that cBioPortal does not.
- **GDC and other repositories** hold raw and controlled-access germline data. cBioPortal is an exploration tool for processed data.
- **Other cancer genomics portals** may offer driver or germline annotations that cBioPortal does not. I haven't checked these against current versions.

**Where cBioPortal fits:** It is useful for exploring germline and somatic calls side by side with clinical data, for example, comparing germline carriers across subtypes or outcomes in studies that include germline calls. It is not a clinical variant-interpretation tool.

**Future support:** Current features and plans are published by the cBioPortal project. Check its documentation at https://docs.cbioportal.org or its GitHub repository for announcements.

If you want to explore germline data now, you can start with a study that has many germline calls, such as [lung_nci_2022](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lung_nci_2022) or [brca_tcga_pub](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=brca_tcga_pub). I can also run a germline frequency query for a specific gene if you tell me which one.
The answer describes cBioPortal as supporting germline variants (mutation_status GERMLINE, 16 studies identified) and notes that most standard features work for both somatic and germline data. However, the notes require the answer to explain that most features (clinical correlation, study view, plots, group comparison) work the same as for somatic data and to point to docs on building a germline study. The answer does mention that mutation frequency, protein domain views, clinical correlation, gene panel coverage, OncoPrint, and mutation diagrams work for both, but it frames cBioPortal as 'designed mainly for somatic data' and emphasizes limitations rather than the parity of features. Critically, it does not point to documentation on building a germline study (the read_guide tool was called for a germline-guide but its content was not surfaced in the answer). The notes also say the answer must not describe cBioPortal as somatic-only, and while it doesn't say somatic-only, it does say 'built mainly for somatic data' and lists many features as not working for germline, which is misleading per the notes. The answer fails to meet the requirement to point to docs on building a germline study and undersells germline support contrary to the notes.

The best route depends on where the data is going. Tell me which of these fits:

1. **Submit to the public cBioPortal.** This goes through the cBioPortal data curation process. Submissions follow the cBioPortal datahub study format, so start with the curators and the format documentation at https://docs.cbioportal.org.
2. **Run or populate a private cBioPortal instance.** You need a complete study package: clinical patient and sample files, a sample list, the molecular data files, and a metadata file for each. The study then loads into your instance like any other study.
3. **Look at your own data without a full submission.** Standalone tools such as OncoPrint and Mutation Mapper can display data without loading it into a full study.

The cBioPortal documentation describes the exact clinical file layout (header rows, attribute datatypes, and the meta file that points to each data file). That layout is general knowledge from me, not something I checked against the database, so confirm the details against the current docs before you build a package.

#88 Out of scopeOut of scope · All Studieswhat do idh1 mutations do? ✓8s
Notes
A correct answer must: clearly separate what comes from cBioPortal data from general background knowledge, and offer cBioPortal-grounded lookups (frequencies, co-mutations, outcomes). Must not: present general biology as if it came from cBioPortal, without a disclaimer.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/57

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 8s · 57,559 tok · $0.001 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · trace

IDH1 encodes isocitrate dehydrogenase 1, a cytosolic enzyme that converts isocitrate to α-ketoglutarate (α-KG) while producing NADPH. The information below is general background knowledge, not data from cBioPortal.

**What the mutations do**

- **Recurrent hotspot:** Most IDH1 mutations are at arginine 132 (R132H is the most common), and they occur in a subset of gliomas, acute myeloid leukemia, and cholangiocarcinoma.
- **Neomorphic gain of function:** The mutant enzyme loses its normal activity and gains the ability to reduce α-KG to D-2-hydroxyglutarate (2-HG).
- **Oncometabolite accumulation:** 2-HG builds up to high levels and inhibits α-KG-dependent enzymes, including TET DNA demethylases and histone demethylases.
- **Downstream effects:** Inhibition of these enzymes is thought to cause widespread DNA and histone hypermethylation (a CpG island methylator phenotype) and a block in normal cell differentiation. This is the proposed mechanism linking IDH1 mutation to tumorigenesis.

**Clinical context (general knowledge)**

In gliomas, IDH mutation is a defining feature in the WHO classification and is generally associated with a better prognosis than IDH-wildtype disease. Inhibitors targeting mutant IDH1 have been developed, but I can't advise on treatment.

I can also show you how IDH1 alterations look in cBioPortal. For example, I can pull IDH1 mutation frequencies across studies, or open a glioma study such as `lgggbm_tcga_pub` to view IDH1 mutations alongside clinical outcomes.
The answer clearly separates general biology knowledge from cBioPortal data with an explicit disclaimer ('The information below is general background knowledge, not data from cBioPortal'), and then offers cBioPortal-grounded lookups (frequencies, co-mutations, outcomes in specific studies). It does not present general biology as if it came from cBioPortal. This satisfies the requirement to clearly separate sources and offer cBioPortal-grounded lookups.

#98 Out of scopeOut of scope · msk_chord_2024Can you think of any flaws in the methodology used in the MSK-CHORD paper (Jee et al., Nature 2024)? ✗15s
Notes

Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/69

Unified agent · ✗ Fail 15s · 58,682 tok · $0.002 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · trace

I can raise several methodological concerns, but I'm working from general knowledge of MSK-CHORD and this kind of study. I haven't checked the paper's text or supplement in this conversation, so verify any specific detail against the paper before relying on it.

**1. Targeted panel sequencing limits the genomic measures**
- MSK-IMPACT covers a few hundred genes, not the whole exome or genome. Measures that depend on genome-wide signal, such as tumor mutational burden, mutational signatures, and homologous recombination deficiency (HRD) scores, are estimated from a limited sample of the genome. Their accuracy and comparability depend on panel size and coverage.
- The IMPACT panel was redesigned several times (roughly 341, 410, 468, and 505 genes). If the analysis pools samples across panel versions without adjusting for this, the denominators and detection rates shift over time, which can look like a biological trend.
- Mutational signature fitting on a small number of mutations per sample is unstable. Signatures with low exposure may be unreliable.

**2. Selection and sampling bias**
- MSK is a tertiary referral center. Its patients may differ from the general population in stage, prior treatment, and access to sequencing. Sequencing is also ordered at clinicians' discretion, so the cohort is not a random sample of each cancer type.
- Longitudinal analyses depend on patients who had repeat biopsies or sequencing. Those patients are usually sicker or have had more treatment, so the sampled timepoints and sites are not representative. Treatment-induced changes can then be confused with natural clonal evolution.

**3. Confounding in pan-cancer and survival analyses**
- Pooling 40+ cancer types lets tissue of origin drive apparent associations between genes or signatures. Co-occurrence and mutual exclusivity results need stratification or mixed models by cancer type to be interpretable.
- Survival comparisons are retrospective and across a long period. Changes in standard of care, follow-up length, and censoring can all differ between groups. Adjustment for stage, treatment, and performance status needs to be checked.
- Immortal-time bias is a risk when defining "altered" status or treatment exposure after diagnosis.
- With many cancer types and genes tested, the multiple-testing correction and whether it was applied across the full set of comparisons should be checked.

**4. Circularity and annotation dependence**
- Driver status and actionability depend on OncoKB and similar knowledge bases, which are built largely from well-studied genes. Analyses that compare "driver" alterations may reflect annotation coverage as much as biology.
- If HRD or other classifiers were trained or validated on different platforms or cancer types, their transfer to panel data needs explicit validation.

**5. Clinical or ML modeling (if present in the paper)**
- Risk models need time-based or external validation, not just random splits, to avoid leakage. Check whether the reported performance was tested on data that was not used for training or tuning.

**What I'd check in the paper:** the handling of panel versions, whether the survival models were adjusted for treatment and stage, whether the co-occurrence analyses were stratified by cancer type, and how multiple testing was corrected.

I can also check the cohort itself in the cBioPortal database (the `msk_chord_2024` study). I could look at sample counts by cancer type, the distribution of sequencing panels, and how many patients have longitudinal samples, which would show how much these concerns apply to the data.
The answer extensively critiques the MSK-CHORD paper's methodology, which the notes explicitly prohibit. It must decline methodology critique as out of scope and offer to explore the cBioPortal data instead. While it briefly mentions the cBioPortal database at the end, the bulk of the response is a detailed methodological critique, violating the 'must not critique the paper's methodology' requirement.

I can help with this. Before I write the full version, I need your requirements. In the meantime, here are the MSK-CHORD facts that will shape the design, followed by a runnable starting skeleton.

**Study facts that affect the design**
- **Cohort:** `msk_chord_2024` has 24,950 patients and 25,040 samples. Only 90 patients have more than one sample, so you can treat each row as one patient.
- **Cancer types:** There are exactly five: NSCLC, colorectal, breast, prostate, and pancreatic. There is no melanoma, so any filter for other types will return nothing.
- **No therapy-response variable:** There is no RECIST or objective-response field. The only outcome proxies are OS and NLP-derived radiology progression events, so any immunotherapy-outcome panel should be labelled as a proxy.
- **Unpaired sample types:** Only 26 patients have both Primary and Metastasis samples. A primary-vs-metastasis view should be an unpaired comparison of all samples.
- **Gene panels:** Coverage varies across four IMPACT versions (341 to 505 genes). Any mutation frequency needs a gene-specific denominator of samples profiled for that gene.
- **License:** The data is CC BY-NC-ND 4.0. A dashboard you share outside research use will need to respect that.

**Starter skeleton** (reads a CSV of clinical fields that you export from cBioPortal; it does not yet pull mutation data)

```python
"""
MSK-CHORD clinicogenomic dashboard (Bokeh skeleton).
Run:  bokeh serve --show msk_chord_dashboard.py
Input: CSV with columns patient_id, cancer_type, sample_type, age, tmb, msi_type
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import logging
import os
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from bokeh.io import curdoc
from bokeh.layouts import column, row
from bokeh.models import (ColumnDataSource, Div, MultiSelect, RangeSlider,
                          Select)
from bokeh.plotting import figure

logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)
log = logging.getLogger("msk_chord")

DATA_PATH = Path(os.environ.get("MSK_CHORD_CSV", "msk_chord_clinical.csv"))
REQUIRED_COLUMNS = {"patient_id", "cancer_type", "sample_type", "age", "tmb", "msi_type"}


def load_data(path: Path) -> pd.DataFrame:
    df = pd.read_csv(path)
    missing = REQUIRED_COLUMNS - set(df.columns)
    if missing:
        raise ValueError(f"{path} is missing columns: {sorted(missing)}")
    df["age"] = pd.to_numeric(df["age"], errors="coerce")
    df["tmb"] = pd.to_numeric(df["tmb"], errors="coerce")
    df["sample_type"] = df["sample_type"].fillna("Unknown")
    df["msi_type"] = df["msi_type"].fillna("NA")
    log.info("Loaded %d rows from %s", len(df), path)
    return df


def build_app(df: pd.DataFrame):
    cancer_options = ["All"] + sorted(df["cancer_type"].dropna().unique())
    sample_options = sorted(df["sample_type"].unique())
    age_min = float(np.floor(df["age"].min()))
    age_max = float(np.ceil(df["age"].max()))

    cancer_sel = Select(title="Cancer type", value="All", options=cancer_options)
    sample_sel = MultiSelect(title="Sample type", value=sample_options,
                             options=sample_options, size=5)
    age_sel = RangeSlider(title="Age (years)", start=age_min, end=age_max,
                          value=(age_min, age_max), step=1)

    summary = Div(width=400)
    tmb_src = ColumnDataSource(dict(top=[], left=[], right=[]))
    msi_src = ColumnDataSource(dict(msi=[], count=[]))

    tmb_fig = figure(title="TMB distribution (nonsynonymous mutations/Mb)",
                     height=320, width=600, tools="pan,wheel_zoom,reset,save")
    tmb_fig.quad(top="top", bottom=0, left="left", right="right",
                 source=tmb_src, fill_color="#4C78A8", line_color="white")
    tmb_fig.yaxis.axis_label = "Patients"

    msi_fig = figure(title="MSI type (patients)", height=320, width=600,
                     x_range=[], tools="pan,reset,save")
    msi_fig.vbar(x="msi", top="count", width=0.7, source=msi_src,
                 color="#F58518")
    msi_fig.yaxis.axis_label = "Patients"

    def filtered() -> pd.DataFrame:
        d = df
        if cancer_sel.value != "All":
            d = d[d["cancer_type"] == cancer_sel.value]
        d = d[d["sample_type"].isin(sample_sel.value)]
        lo, hi = age_sel.value
        return d[d["age"].between(lo, hi) | d["age"].isna()]

    def update(attr, old, new):
        d = filtered()
        tmb = d["tmb"].dropna()
        if len(tmb):
            counts, edges = np.histogram(tmb, bins=40)
            tmb_src.data = dict(top=counts, left=edges[:-1], right=edges[1:])
        else:
            tmb_src.data = dict(top=[], left=[], right=[])

        msi = d["msi_type"].value_counts()
        msi_src.data = dict(msi=list(msi.index), count=list(msi.values))
        msi_fig.x_range.factors = list(msi.index)

        summary.text = (f"{len(d):,} patients shown "
                        f"({d['tmb'].notna().sum():,} with TMB)")

    for widget in (cancer_sel, sample_sel, age_sel):
        widget.on_change("value", update)
    update(None, None, None)

    controls = column(cancer_sel, sample_sel, age_sel, summary, width=300)
    return row(controls, column(tmb_fig, msi_fig))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    pass  # Use `bokeh serve`; the app is built below.

if __name__.startswith("bokeh_app") or __name__ == "__main__" or True:
    curdoc().add_root(build_app(load_data(DATA_PATH)))
    curdoc().title = "MSK-CHORD Dashboard"
```

Known limitations of this skeleton:
- The "age" filter keeps patients with missing age. Confirm that age is not floored or capped for de-identification before relying on it.
- The `if __name__` block at the bottom is messy and should be cleaned up in the production version.
- It does not include genomic data yet. A gene-level mutation view would need a per-gene denominator built from the panel data.

**What I need from you to write the full version**
1. **Users and purpose:** Is this for exploratory analysis, a teaching tool, or a shared internal tool? This determines whether it needs authentication, caching, and deployment config.
2. **Genomic views:** Which genes should it cover? Should it show mutation frequencies, co-occurrence, or OncoPrint-style summaries, and should it use per-gene denominators?
3. **Clinical stratifiers:** Which variables should users filter and group by (for example, cancer type, sample type, MSI, PD-L1, age, sex, stage)?
4. **Survival:** Do you want Kaplan-Meier curves? If so, I'd use `lifelines` for the fitting and Bokeh only for rendering. Bokeh has no built-in KM fitting.
5. **Data source:** Should the app pull live from the public cBioPortal REST API, or read a pre-exported snapshot? A snapshot is more reproducible for production.
6. **Stats:** Should the dashboard show p-values or hazard ratios? I'd compute those in Python with `scipy` or `lifelines` and label them clearly, since the database itself can't run statistical tests.
7. **Deployment:** Will it run as a `bokeh serve` app behind a reverse proxy, in Docker, or on a Bokeh server cluster?

Send me your list and I'll write the full, modular version with tests and a README.
The answer generates substantial Bokeh dashboard code (a full Python skeleton with imports, data loading, widgets, and figures) and explicitly offers to write a 'full, modular version with tests and a README.' This directly violates the requirement to decline building external application code. The notes are clear: must not generate complex external pipeline or dashboard code (Bokeh, PySpark, lifelines), and must decline and point to cBioPortal API/built-in views instead.

Unified agent · ✗ Fail 23s · 121,865 tok · $0.004 · 2 LLM / 2 tool calls · trace

Here's a script that reads the MSK-CHORD timeline files (`data_timeline_*.txt`) from an unzipped study download and provides helpers for filtering, per-patient timelines, and summaries.

Download and unzip the study from the cBioPortal datahub (`msk_chord_2024.tar.gz`). I couldn't inspect the file listing from here, so the script finds every `data_timeline_*.txt` in the folder rather than assuming specific names.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Query MSK-CHORD timeline files (data_timeline_*.txt) from an unzipped
cBioPortal study folder (msk_chord_2024/).

Requires: pandas
    pip install pandas
"""

import argparse
import glob
import os
from io import StringIO

import pandas as pd

PATIENT_COL = "PATIENT_ID"
START_COL = "START_DATE"   # days from diagnosis in cBioPortal timeline files
STOP_COL = "STOP_DATE"
EVENT_COL = "EVENT_TYPE"


def _read_tsv_skip_comments(path: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Read a cBioPortal TSV, skipping '#' metadata lines. Keeps all values as strings."""
    with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as fh:
        body = "".join(line for line in fh if not line.startswith("#"))
    return pd.read_csv(StringIO(body), sep="\t", dtype=str, keep_default_na=False)


def load_timeline(study_dir: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Load and concatenate all timeline files in a study folder into one long table."""
    paths = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(study_dir, "data_timeline_*.txt")))
    if not paths:
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"No data_timeline_*.txt files found in {study_dir!r}")

    frames = []
    for path in paths:
        df = _read_tsv_skip_comments(path)
        df["SOURCE_FILE"] = os.path.basename(path)
        frames.append(df)

    timeline = pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True, sort=False)

    # Numeric versions of the day-offset columns (blank -> NaN)
    for col in (START_COL, STOP_COL):
        if col in timeline.columns:
            timeline[f"{col}_NUM"] = pd.to_numeric(timeline[col], errors="coerce")

    return timeline


def query_events(
    timeline: pd.DataFrame,
    event_type: str | None = None,
    filters: dict[str, str | list[str]] | None = None,
    columns: list[str] | None = None,
) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """
    Filter timeline events.

    event_type: e.g. "Treatment" (matched case-insensitively against EVENT_TYPE)
    filters:    {column: value or [values]} — exact match, case-sensitive, e.g.
                {"SUBTYPE": "Immuno", "AGENT": "PEMBROLIZUMAB"}
    columns:    optional list of columns to return
    """
    df = timeline
    if event_type is not None and EVENT_COL in df.columns:
        df = df[df[EVENT_COL].str.lower() == event_type.lower()]

    for col, val in (filters or {}).items():
        if col not in df.columns:
            raise KeyError(f"Column {col!r} not in timeline. Available: {list(df.columns)}")
        vals = [val] if isinstance(val, str) else list(val)
        df = df[df[col].isin(vals)]

    if columns:
        df = df[columns]
    return df.reset_index(drop=True)


def patient_timeline(timeline: pd.DataFrame, patient_id: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """All timeline events for one patient, ordered by START_DATE (days from diagnosis)."""
    df = timeline[timeline[PATIENT_COL] == patient_id].copy()
    sort_cols = [c for c in (f"{START_COL}_NUM", EVENT_COL) if c in df.columns]
    return df.sort_values(sort_cols, na_position="last").reset_index(drop=True)


def summarize_treatments(timeline: pd.DataFrame, by: str = "AGENT") -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Patient counts per treatment value (e.g. AGENT or SUBTYPE) from Treatment events."""
    tx = query_events(timeline, event_type="Treatment")
    out = (
        tx.groupby(by)[PATIENT_COL]
        .nunique()
        .rename("patients")
        .sort_values(ascending=False)
        .reset_index()
    )
    return out


def main() -> None:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Query MSK-CHORD timeline files.")
    parser.add_argument("study_dir", help="Path to unzipped msk_chord_2024 folder")
    parser.add_argument("--event-type", help="e.g. Treatment, Diagnosis, Sample acquisition")
    parser.add_argument("--filter", action="append", default=[],
                        help="COLUMN=VALUE (repeatable), e.g. --filter SUBTYPE=Immuno")
    parser.add_argument("--patient", help="Show full timeline for one PATIENT_ID")
    parser.add_argument("--summary-by", help="Patient counts by column, e.g. AGENT or SUBTYPE")
    parser.add_argument("--out", help="Optional path to write results as TSV")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    timeline = load_timeline(args.study_dir)
    print(f"Loaded {len(timeline):,} timeline rows from "
          f"{timeline['SOURCE_FILE'].nunique()} file(s); "
          f"{timeline[PATIENT_COL].nunique():,} patients.")

    if args.patient:
        result = patient_timeline(timeline, args.patient)
    elif args.summary_by:
        result = summarize_treatments(timeline, by=args.summary_by)
    else:
        filters = dict(f.split("=", 1) for f in args.filter)
        result = query_events(timeline, event_type=args.event_type, filters=filters)

    print(f"\n{len(result):,} row(s)")
    print(result.head(50).to_string(index=False))

    if args.out:
        result.to_csv(args.out, sep="\t", index=False)
        print(f"\nWrote {args.out}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

**Usage examples**

```bash
# Count patients per treatment subtype
python chord_timeline.py msk_chord_2024 --summary-by SUBTYPE

# Immunotherapy events
python chord_timeline.py msk_chord_2024 --event-type Treatment --filter SUBTYPE=Immuno --out immuno.tsv

# Full timeline for one patient
python chord_timeline.py msk_chord_2024 --patient P-0000123
```

**Python use in your own code**

```python
from chord_timeline import load_timeline, query_events, patient_timeline, summarize_treatments

tl = load_timeline("msk_chord_2024")
print(tl.columns.tolist())   # confirm the exact column names in your files

immuno = query_events(tl, event_type="Treatment", filters={"SUBTYPE": "Immuno"})
print(summarize_treatments(tl, by="AGENT").head(10))
print(patient_timeline(tl, "P-0000123"))
```

**Notes**
- Check `tl.columns` first. The script assumes the standard cBioPortal names (`PATIENT_ID`, `START_DATE`, `STOP_DATE`, `EVENT_TYPE`), but the other column names come from the files themselves.
- `START_DATE` and `STOP_DATE` are day offsets from diagnosis, so negative values are possible and mean before diagnosis.
- Patient IDs here match `PATIENT_ID` in `data_clinical_patient.txt`.
- The study is licensed CC BY-NC-ND 4.0. Contact datarequests@mskcc.org for commercial use.

If you'd rather query the same events from the cBioPortal ClickHouse database instead of local files, I can write that version too.
The notes require code against the public cBioPortal REST API (e.g. /api/studies/{studyId}/clinical-events) and must not write code needing direct ClickHouse credentials. The answer instead writes code that reads local downloaded flat files, and even offers a ClickHouse version at the end. It does not use the REST API at all, violating the 'must' requirement. Therefore it fails.

**Interpretation:**
- **IDH-wildtype** shows the clearest signal. Many genes are high in roughly 35–50% of samples, with mean z-scores around 1.2–1.8, so the whole distribution shifts upward rather than only a few outliers.
- **IDH-mutant codeleted and non-codeleted** show weaker signals. The top genes are high in at most about 16% of samples, and several have mean z-scores near zero (e.g., OR7D2, GJA3). These are likely driven by a subset of tumors and should be treated as candidates, not established subtype markers.
- Z-scores are relative to this cohort, so "overexpressed" means high compared with all lower grade gliomas here, not compared with normal brain tissue.

**Short answer:** The data suggest the two groups differ, with EGFR-amplified tumors showing more deaths so far. I can't test whether that difference is statistically significant, so the formal comparison needs the Survival tab.

**Cohort:** TCGA Lower Grade Glioma (PanCancer Atlas, 514 samples). Counts are patients with overall survival (OS) data. The groups are mutually exclusive, so an IDH1-mutant tumor with EGFR amplification falls in its own small group.

| Group | Patients | OS events (deceased) | Censored (alive at last follow-up) | Event rate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IDH1 mutant only | 392 | 72 | 320 | 18% |
| EGFR amplified only | 37 | 24 | 13 | 65% |
| IDH1 mutant + EGFR amplified | 2 | 0 | 2 | too few to interpret |
| Neither | 82 | 29 | 53 | 35% |

- **IDH1-mutant:** Most patients are still censored, and the longest follow-up is about 182 months. Fewer than half have had an event, so a Kaplan-Meier median is likely not reached.
- **EGFR-amplified only:** Most patients have had an event, so a Kaplan-Meier median may be estimable.
- **Caveat:** Follow-up times differ a lot between groups, which affects the raw event rates. Median survival and hazard ratios require Kaplan-Meier and Cox models, which I can't compute here.

**Survival comparison (Kaplan-Meier curves and log-rank test):**
[Compare IDH1-altered vs EGFR-altered survival](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/comparison/survival?cancer_study_list=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=IDH1%3A+MUT%0AEGFR%3A+AMP&case_set_id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&comparison_selectedGroups=%5B%22IDH1%22%2C%22EGFR%22%2C%22IDH1%3A+MUT%22%2C%22EGFR%3A+AMP%22%5D)

|---|---|---|---|

| Lower-grade glioma | `lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018` | 35 / 514 | 6.8% |

- **Lung:** EGFR mutations are about four times more frequent in adenocarcinoma than in squamous cell carcinoma. Pooled across both histologies, the rate is 84 / 1,050 (8.0%).
- **Brain:** Glioblastoma has the highest EGFR mutation rate of the four types. Lower-grade glioma is much lower, at 6.8%.
- **Overall:** EGFR mutation is more common in glioblastoma than in any lung histology here. Mutation rates in the two lung subtypes differ by more than the lung-versus-brain gap in lower-grade glioma.

The profiled denominators come from the precomputed frequency view. The glioblastoma denominator (397) is smaller than the study's 592 samples, so not every sample was profiled for EGFR.

I did not run a significance test. These are descriptive frequencies only. The mutation-type breakdown is not included here.
